.

4.7.2.
Optimal Inference in Predictive Regressions with Persistent Regressors

Following
Jansson and Moreira, 2006
consider the case where
\left\{(y_{t},x_{t}^{\prime}\right\}
is generated by the predictive regression model with the local to unity specification for the autocorrelation matrix.

Let
\hat{\Omega}
be a consistent estimator of

\displaystyle\Omega=\begin{bmatrix}\omega_{yy}&\omega_{yx}\\
\omega_{xy}&\omega_{xx}\end{bmatrix}=\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{lim}}T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{s=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}\left[\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon_{yt}\\
\Psi(L)\epsilon_{xt}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon_{ys}\\
\Psi(L)\epsilon_{xs}\end{pmatrix}^{\prime}\right],

(4.192)

which is the long-run variance of
\left(\epsilon_{yt},\Psi(L)\epsilon_{xt}\right)^{\prime}
.

Example 21
.

Consider the general I(1) vector process examined by
Phillips and Durlauf, 1986

\displaystyle x_{t}=x_{t-1}+v_{t},\ \ \text{where}\ \ x_{0}=0,

(4.193)

and
v_{t}
is a weakly stationary stochastic process with unconditional variance
\mathbb{E}\left(v_{t}v_{t}^{\prime}\right)=G
and long run covariance matrix
\Omega=G+\Lambda+\Lambda^{\prime}
. Notice that the matrix
\Lambda
has the following representation

\displaystyle\Lambda=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(v_{0}v_{j}^{\prime}\right)

(4.194)

By considering the case of non-IID errors
x_{T}/\sqrt{T}
converges to the vector Brownian motion BM
(\Omega)
:

\displaystyle T^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{[Tr]}v_{t}\Rightarrow B(r).

(4.195)

Then, we obtain that

\displaystyle T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}v_{t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\int_{0}^{1}B(r)dB(r)^{\prime}+\Lambda,

(4.196)

\displaystyle T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t}v_{t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\int_{0}^{1}B(r)dB(r)^{\prime}+G+\Lambda.

(4.197)

Example 22
.

(Cointegration in Systems of Equations) Consider the following DGP

\displaystyle y_{t}

\displaystyle=\beta z_{t}+v_{t}

(4.198)

\displaystyle v_{t}

\displaystyle=\rho v_{t-1}+\epsilon_{1t}

(4.199)

\displaystyle z_{t}

\displaystyle=z_{t-1}+\epsilon_{2t}

(4.200)

and

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\epsilon_{1t}\\
\epsilon_{2t}\end{pmatrix}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{1}^{2}&\theta\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\\
\theta\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}&\sigma_{2}^{2}\end{pmatrix}\right)

(4.201)

Moreover, the system can be represented using the usual ECM form

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\Delta y_{t}\\
\Delta z_{t}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\rho-1&\beta(1-\rho)\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}y_{t-1}\\
z_{t-1}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}u_{1t}\\
u_{2t}\end{bmatrix}

(4.202)

where
u_{1t}=\beta\epsilon_{2t}+\epsilon_{1t},u_{2t}=\epsilon_{2t}
, and
\mathbb{E}\left(uu^{\top}\right)=\Lambda
, with

\displaystyle\Lambda=\begin{bmatrix}\beta^{2}\sigma_{2}^{2}+\sigma_{1}^{2}+2\beta\theta\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}&\beta\sigma_{2}^{2}+\theta\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}\\
\beta^{2}\sigma_{2}^{2}+\theta\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}&\sigma_{2}^{2}\end{bmatrix}

(4.203)

Therefore, within our setting we aim to compare the efficiency of the likelihood ratio test and the Wald type statistics as structural break detectors for the predictive regression model with persistent regressors at an unknown break-point. In order to study the asymptotic behaviour of the individual components we consider limit theorems involving invariance principles for partial sums within the moderate deviations framework developed by
Phillips and Magdalinos, 2007
.

Notice that the stochastic process
x_{\lfloor ns\rfloor,n}:=x_{\lfloor ns\rfloor}/d_{n}
on the Skorohod space
\mathcal{D}[0,1]
converges weakly to a Gaussian process
G(s)
. Furthermore, on a suitably expanded probability space there exists a process
\left(x_{t,n}^{0},1\leq t\leq n\right)=_{d}\left(x_{t,n},1\leq t\leq n\right)
such that

\displaystyle\underset{0\leq s\leq 1}{\text{sup}}\left|x_{\lfloor ns\rfloor,n}^{0}-G(s)\right|=o_{p}(1)

4.7.3.
Moderate Deviations from a Unit Root in Autoregressive Processes

Example 23
.

Consider the autoregressive time series model (see,
Phillips and Magdalinos, 2005
)

\displaystyle x_{t}=\rho_{n}x_{t-1}+\epsilon_{t},\ \ t\in\left\{1,...,n\right\}

(4.204)

such that the autocorrelation coefficient is defined as

\displaystyle\rho_{n}=\left(1+\frac{c}{n^{\gamma}}\right),\ \ \gamma\in(0,1)

(4.205)

Consider the component random elements
x_{[n^{\gamma}\bullet]}
of the Skorohod space. Furthermore, denote with

\displaystyle W_{n^{\gamma}}(\bullet):=\frac{1}{n^{\gamma/2}}\sum_{j=1}^{[n^{\gamma}\bullet]}\epsilon_{j}

(4.206)

It is possible to approximate the partial sum process on
\mathcal{D}[0,+\infty)
for
x_{[n^{\gamma}\bullet]}
by the Stieltjes integral

\displaystyle U_{n^{\gamma}}(\bullet):=\int_{0}^{\bullet}e^{c(\bullet-r)}dW_{n^{\gamma}}(r)=\frac{1}{n^{\gamma/2}}\sum_{i=1}^{[n^{\gamma}\bullet]}e^{\frac{c}{n^{\gamma}}\left(n^{\gamma}\bullet-j\right)}\epsilon_{j}.

(4.207)

Therefore for each
\gamma\in(0,1)
and
c<0
, we have that

\displaystyle\underset{t\in[0,n^{1-\gamma}]}{\text{sup}}\left|\frac{1}{n^{\gamma/2}}x_{[n^{\gamma}t]}-U_{n^{\gamma}}(t)\right|=o_{p}(1)\ \ \ \text{as}\ \ \ n\to\infty.

(4.208)

Remark 21
.

Therefore, by considering the above invariance law we can operate in the familiar framework of
Phillips, 1987b
where
U_{n^{\gamma}}(t)
, and hence the time series
x_{n}
with appropriate normalization, converges to the linear diffusion
\int_{0}^{t}e^{c(t-s)}dW(s)
, where
W
is Brownian motion with variance
\sigma^{2}
. Theorem 4.6 in the paper of PM provides a bridge between stationary and local to unity autoregressions with weakly dependent innovation errors. Furthermore, when the innovation error sequence is a linear process, then the least squares estimator has been found to satisfy a Gaussian limit theory with an asymptotic bias.

Notice that with the seminal paper of
Phillips and Magdalinos, 2007
, the authors introduce limit theorems and invariance principles for moderate deviations from unit root. The particular framework is employed in time series models such as autoregressive and predictive regression models with predictors assumed to be generated with the LUR specification. A strong approximation over the interval
[0,n^{1-\alpha}]
for the partial sum process of
i.i.d
errors can be constructed via an expanded probability space with a Brownian motion
W(.)
with variance
\sigma^{2}
for which

\displaystyle\underset{t\in[0,n^{1-\alpha}]}{\text{sup}}\left|W_{n^{\alpha}}-W(t)\right|=o_{a.s}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-\frac{1}{\nu}}}\right)

(4.209)

Therefore, we have that one of the main results of the paper which provide a uniform approximation of
n^{-\alpha/2}y_{\lfloor n^{\alpha}.\rfloor}
by
V_{n^{\alpha}}
on
[0,n^{1-\alpha}]
. For each
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
c<0
we have that

\displaystyle\underset{t\in[0,n^{1-\alpha}]}{\text{sup}}\ \left|\frac{1}{n^{\alpha/2}}y_{\lfloor n^{\alpha}t\rfloor}-V_{n^{\alpha}}(t)\right|=o_{p}(1),\ \ \text{as}\ n\to\infty

(4.210)

The importance of the above result lies in the fact that an embedding of the random element
V_{n^{\alpha}}(t)
to the linear diffusion
J_{c}(t):=\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}e^{c(t-r)}dB(r)
is possible. Using integration by parts it can be shown that,

\displaystyle\underset{t\in[0,n^{1-\alpha}]}{\text{sup}}\ \left|V_{n^{\alpha}}(t)-J_{c}(t)\right|\leq 2\underset{t\in[0,n^{1-\alpha}]}{\text{sup}}\ \left|B_{n^{\alpha}}(t)-B(t)\right|.

(4.211)

Moreover, by Lemma 3.1 we obtain the following expression

\displaystyle\underset{t\in[0,n^{1-\alpha}]}{\text{sup}}\ \left|V_{n^{\alpha}}(t)-J_{c}(t)\right|=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-\frac{1}{\nu}}}\right),\ \ \text{as}\ \ n\to\infty

(4.212)

Therefore, we obtain that

\displaystyle\underset{t\in[0,n^{1-\alpha}]}{\text{sup}}\ \left|\frac{1}{n^{\alpha/2}}y_{\lfloor n^{\alpha}t\rfloor}-J_{c}(t)\right|=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-\frac{1}{\nu}}}\right),\ \ \text{as}\ \ n\to\infty.

(4.213)

We note that the limit theory is established through a combination of a functional law to a diffusion and a central limit law to a Gaussian random variable. A more immediate consequence is the limit law of the random element
y_{\lfloor n^{\alpha}.\rfloor}
on the original probability space. For all
j=0,...,\lfloor n^{1-\alpha}\rfloor-1
and
p\in[0,1]
we obtain
\frac{1}{n^{\alpha/2}}y_{\lfloor n^{\alpha}j\rfloor+\lfloor n^{\alpha}p\rfloor}
.

Lemma 9
.

(Lemma 5.1 in
Phillips and Magdalinos, 2005
) For each
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
c>0
, we have that

(a)

\displaystyle\underset{t\in[0,n^{1-\alpha}]}{\text{sup}}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{n}^{-n^{\alpha}s}dB_{n^{\alpha}}(s)-\int_{0}^{t}e^{-cs}dB(s)\right|=o_{p}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-\frac{1}{\nu}}}\right)

(4.214)

(b)

\displaystyle\underset{t\in[0,n^{1-\alpha}]}{\text{sup}}\left|\int_{0}^{t}\rho_{n}^{-\left(\lfloor n^{\alpha}t\rfloor-\lfloor n^{\alpha}s\rfloor\right)}dB_{n^{\alpha}}(s)-J_{-c}(t)\right|=o_{p}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-\frac{1}{\nu}}}\right)

(4.215)

as
n\to\infty
, on the same probability space.

We analyze each of the two terms separately. The term containing the block components can be written:

\displaystyle U_{1n}

\displaystyle=\rho_{n}^{-2\kappa_{n}}\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor n^{1-\alpha}\rfloor-1}\frac{1}{n^{2\alpha}}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor n^{\alpha}\rfloor}y^{2}_{\lfloor n^{\alpha}j\rfloor+k}

\displaystyle=\rho_{n}^{-2\kappa_{n}}\int_{0}^{\lfloor n^{1-\alpha}\rfloor}\left(\int_{0}^{r}\rho_{n}^{\lfloor n^{\alpha}r\rfloor-n^{\alpha}s}dB_{n^{\alpha}}(s)\right)^{2}dr+o_{p}(1).

Taking the inner integral along
[0,r]=[0,\lfloor n^{1-\alpha}\rfloor]
we have, up to
o_{p}(1)
that

\displaystyle U_{1n}

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{0}^{\lfloor n^{1-\alpha}\rfloor}\rho_{n}^{-n^{\alpha}s}dB_{n^{\alpha}}(s)\right)^{2}\rho_{n}^{-2\kappa_{n}}\int_{0}^{\lfloor n^{1-\alpha}\rfloor}\rho_{n}^{2\lfloor n^{\alpha}r\rfloor}dr+R_{n},

(4.216)

where the remainder term
R_{n}
is shown in the Appendix to be
o_{p}(1)
.

The second integral on the right side can be evaluated directly to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\lfloor n^{1-\alpha}\rfloor}\rho_{n}^{2\lfloor n^{\alpha}r\rfloor}dr=\frac{\rho_{n}^{2\kappa_{n}}}{2c}\left[1+O(1)\right],\ \ \text{as}\ \ n\to\infty.

(4.217)

Furthermore, we obtain that

\displaystyle U_{1n}=\frac{1}{2c}\left(\int_{0}^{\lfloor n^{1-\alpha}\rfloor}e^{-cs}dB(s)\right)^{2}+o_{p}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-\frac{1}{\nu}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2c}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-cs}dB(s)\right)^{2}+o_{p}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-\frac{1}{\nu}}}\right)

Then,
\Psi_{n}^{\prime}\to_{\text{a.s}}\Psi=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\rho^{-j}u_{j}^{\prime}
, and it follows by the Shorokhod representation theorem that
\Psi_{n}\to_{d}\Psi
. Then, by joint weak convergence of
\Psi_{n}
and
Z_{n}
it follows that
\left(Z_{n},\Psi_{n}\right)\Rightarrow\left(Z,\Psi\right)
as
n\to\infty
, with
Z=_{d}\Psi
. The limiting random variables
\Psi
and
Z
can be shown to be independent by modifying Anderson’s argument adjusted for weakly dependent errors. The idea is that, as
n\to\infty
,
Z_{n}
can be approximated by the first
\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor
elements of the sum
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\rho^{-j}u_{j}
whereas
\Psi_{n}
can be approximated by the last
\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor
elements of the sum
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\rho^{-(n-j)-1}u_{j}
, where
\left(L_{n}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence increasing to
\infty
with
L_{n}\leq n/3
for each
n
.

Accordingly, we define

\displaystyle Z_{n}^{*}:=\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor}\rho^{-j}u_{j}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \Psi_{n}^{*}:=\sum_{j=n-\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor+1}^{n}\rho^{-(n-j)}u_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor-1}\rho^{-k}u_{n-k+1}.

(4.218)

We may further approximate

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}^{*}=\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor-1}\rho^{-k}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}c_{s}\epsilon_{n-k+1-s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor-1}\rho^{-k}\sum_{s=0}^{\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor}c_{s}\epsilon_{n-k+1-s}+\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor-1}\rho^{-k}\sum_{s=\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor+1}^{\infty}c_{s}\epsilon_{n-k+1-s}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{n}^{**}+\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor-1}\rho^{-k}\sum_{s=\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor+1}^{\infty}c_{s}\epsilon_{n-k+1-s},

We have that,
\Psi_{n}-\Psi_{n}^{*}=\sum_{k=\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor}^{n}\rho^{-k}u_{n-k+1}
, and so

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|\Psi_{n}-\Psi_{n}^{*}\right|\leq\mathbb{E}\left|u_{1}\right|\sum_{k=\lfloor L_{n}\rfloor+1}^{n}|\rho|^{-k}=\mathcal{O}\left(|\rho|\right)^{-L_{n}}

(4.219)

Similar to the sample variance, the asymptotic behaviour of the sample covariance is partly determined by elements of the time series
y_{t-1}u_{t}
that do not belong to the block components

\displaystyle\bigg\{y_{\lfloor n^{\alpha}j\rfloor+k-1}u_{\lfloor n^{\alpha}j\rfloor+k}:j=0,...,\lfloor n^{1-\alpha}\rfloor-1,k=1,...,\lfloor n^{\alpha}\rfloor\bigg\}

(4.220)

Obtaining limits for the block components and the remaining time series separately in a method similar to that used for the sample variance will work. For the limit as
\alpha\to 1
, we have that
n^{1-\alpha}\to 1
, and so
\lfloor n^{1-\alpha}\rfloor=1
for
\alpha=1
, in which case
j=0
in the aforementioned blocking scheme. Therefore, the invariance principle proposed by
Phillips, 1987a

n^{-1/2}y_{\lfloor np\rfloor}\Rightarrow J_{c}(p)
on
\mathcal{D}\left([0,1]\right)
which yield the usual local to unity limit result

\displaystyle n\left(\hat{\rho}-\rho\right)\Rightarrow\frac{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}J_{c}(r)dB(r)}{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}J_{c}(r)^{2}dr}.

(4.221)

In other words continuity in the limit theory cannot be achieved at the boundary with the conventional local to unity asympotics, at least without using the blocking construction.

Supplementary Results: Main Proofs

Proposition 8
.

For each
x\in[0,M],M>0
, possibly depending on
n
, and real- valued, measurable function
f
on
[0,\infty)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\alpha/2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n^{\alpha}x\rfloor}f\left(\frac{i}{n^{\alpha}}\right)u_{i}=\int_{0}^{x}f(r)dB_{n^{\alpha}}(r).

(4.222)

An immediate consequence of the above Proposition is the following identity. For each
x\in[0,n^{1-\alpha}]
and
m\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\alpha/2}}\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor n^{\alpha}x\rfloor}f\left(\frac{i}{n^{\alpha}}\right)u_{i+m}=\int_{0}^{x}f(r)dB_{n^{\alpha}}\left(r+\frac{m}{n^{\alpha}}\right).

(4.223)

Proposition 9
.

For each
\alpha\in(0,1)
we have that

\displaystyle\underset{0\leq t\leq n}{\text{max}}\left|\frac{\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}}{n^{\alpha/2}}\right|=o_{p}(1),\ \ \text{as}\ \ n\to\infty.

(4.224)

Proof.

The arguments follows Phillips (1999). Summability conditions of
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}j|c_{j}|
ensures that
\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\tilde{c}_{j}\epsilon_{t-j}
converges absolutely almost surely. Therefore, Fatou’s lemma and the Minkowski inequality give the following

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}|^{\nu}

\displaystyle\leq\underset{N\to\infty}{\text{ lim inf}}\ \mathbb{E}\left|\sum_{j=0}^{N}\tilde{c}_{j}\epsilon_{t-j}\right|\leq\underset{N\to\infty}{\text{ lim inf}}\left[\sum_{j=0}^{N}\big(\mathbb{E}\left|\tilde{c}_{j}\epsilon_{t-j}\right|^{\nu}\big)^{\frac{1}{\nu}}\right]^{\nu}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}|\epsilon_{0}|^{\nu}\underset{N\to\infty}{\text{ lim inf}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\left|\tilde{c}_{j}\right|\right)^{\nu}=\mathbb{E}|\epsilon_{0}|^{\nu}C_{2}^{\nu}

where
C_{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left|\tilde{c}_{j}\right|<\infty
.

Thus, for any
\delta>0
the Markov inequality gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\underset{0\leq t\leq n}{\text{max}}|\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}|>\delta n^{\alpha/2}\right)\leq\sum_{t=0}^{n}\mathbb{P}\left(|\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}|>\delta n^{\alpha/2}\right)\leq\sum_{t=0}^{n}\frac{\mathbb{E}|\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}|^{\nu}}{\delta^{\nu}n^{\nu\alpha/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}|\tilde{\epsilon}_{0}|^{\nu}C_{2}^{\nu}}{\delta^{\nu}}\frac{n+1}{n^{\nu\alpha/2}}=o(1).

if and only if
\frac{\nu\alpha}{2}>1
.
∎

Proposition 10
.

The following two results hold

(a)

Let
y_{nt}^{*}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}
. Then, for each
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}y_{t-1}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{t}=\frac{1}{n^{\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}y^{*}_{nt-1}\widetilde{\epsilon}_{t}+o_{p}(1),\ \ \text{as}\ n\to\infty.

(4.225)

(b)

Let
\gamma_{m}(h)=\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}u_{t-h}\right]=\sigma^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c_{j}\tilde{c}_{j+h}
for
h\geq 0
and
m_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\rho_{n}^{i-1}\gamma_{m}(i)
.
Then it holds that,

\displaystyle\underset{n\to\infty}{\text{lim}}m_{n}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{m}(i).

(4.226)

Proposition 11
.

For part (a), we can write the following expression

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left(y^{*}_{nt-1}-y_{t-1}\right)\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left[\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}\rho_{n}^{i}u_{t-i-1}-y_{0}\rho_{n}^{t}\right)\right]\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\rho_{n}^{i}u_{t-i-1}\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}+o_{p}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}}}\right),

Since, by Proposition A3 and the fact that
\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left|\rho_{n}\right|^{t}=\mathcal{O}\left(n^{\alpha}\right)
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{n^{\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}y_{0}\rho_{n}^{t}\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}\right|\leq\left|\frac{y_{0}}{n^{\alpha/2}}\right|\underset{1\leq t\leq n}{\text{max}}\left|\frac{\tilde{\epsilon}_{t}}{n^{\alpha/2}}\right|\frac{1}{n^{\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left|\rho_{n}\right|^{t}=o_{p}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\frac{1+3\alpha}{2}}}\right).

(4.227)

Proof of Lemma 4.1

Consider the stochastic process

\displaystyle\xi_{t}:=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c_{j}\tilde{c}_{j}\epsilon_{t-j}\epsilon_{t-i}

(4.228)

is a stationary process with autocovariance function given by

\displaystyle\gamma_{\xi}(h)=\sigma^{4}\sum_{j=h}^{\infty}\sum_{i=j+1}^{\infty}c_{j-h}\tilde{c}_{i-h}\tilde{c}_{i},\ \ h\in\mathbb{Z}.

(4.229)

Then by Theorem of Inrangimov and Linnik (1971)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sum_{t=1}^{n}\xi_{t}\right)^{2}\right]\leq n\sum_{h=-\infty}^{\infty}\left|\gamma_{\xi}(h)\right|,

(4.230)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{n^{\frac{1+\alpha}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\xi_{t}\right)^{2}\right]\leq\frac{1}{n^{\alpha}}\sum_{h=-\infty}^{\infty}\left|\gamma_{\xi}(h)\right|<\frac{2}{n^{\alpha}}\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}\left|\gamma_{\xi}(h)\right|=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\alpha}}\right)

(4.231)

provided that
\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}\left|\gamma_{\xi}(h)\right|<\infty
.

To show summability of the covariance function
\gamma_{\xi}
, write

\displaystyle\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}\left|\gamma_{\xi}(h)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sigma^{4}\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=h}^{\infty}\left|c_{j-h}c_{j}\right|\left|\sum_{i=j+1}^{\infty}\tilde{c}_{i-h}\tilde{c}_{i}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sigma^{4}\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=h}^{\infty}\left|c_{j-h}c_{j}\right|\left(\sum_{i=j+1}^{\infty}\tilde{c}_{i-h}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\left(\sum_{i=j+1}^{\infty}\tilde{c}_{i}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\sigma^{4}\left(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\tilde{c}_{i}^{2}\right)\sum_{h=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=h}^{\infty}\left|c_{j-h}\right|\left|c_{j}\right|

\displaystyle=C_{4}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\left|c_{j}\right|\sum_{j=h}^{\infty}\left|c_{h}\right|\leq\sigma^{4}C_{4}C_{1}^{2}<\infty.

Once the fitted stationary process is obtained, the variance
\Sigma
of
\left\{w_{t}\right\}
can be consistently estimated as

\displaystyle\hat{\Sigma}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\hat{w}_{t}\hat{w}_{t}.

(4.232)

Summary

Although it is not shown explicitly, the idea of stationary transformations can be used also for the essentially singular model to construct the regression which yields asympotically invariant, with respect to nuisance parameters, tests.

Lemma 10
.

Suppose that
X_{ni}=\left[1+\frac{z_{n}}{n}\right]X_{n,i-1}+v_{ni}
, for
i=1,...,n,X_{n0}=0
, where
z_{n}\to z
and
v_{ni}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c_{j}(a_{n})e_{i-j}
with
\alpha_{n}\to\alpha
and with the
c_{j}
’s satisfying
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left|c_{j}(\alpha)\right|<\infty
. Then, the stochastic process
n^{-1/2}X_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}
converges in law in the Skorokhod space to
\int_{0}^{t}\text{exp}\left[z(t-s)\right]dW(t)
, where
W(t)
is the two-dimensional Brownian motion with variance given by

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}c_{r}(\alpha)\right)\left(\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}c_{r}(\alpha)\right)^{\prime}

(4.233)

is finite and non-singular.

The use of moderate deviation principles are useful in understanding the various asymptotic theory results that apply across the parameter space of the autoregressive coefficient of the nonstationary autoregressive model. In particular, for the case of quantile autoregressions and quantile predictive regression models a relevant framework is proposed by
Katsouris, 2022a
.

4.7.4.
Multivariate Predictive Regression Model

Consider the multivariate predictive regression model with lag-augmentation

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}_{t}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{y}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{u}_{t},\ \ \ \ \ \ \

(4.234)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{R}_{n}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{t},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{with}\ \ \ \mathbf{R}_{n}=\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\frac{\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}{n^{\alpha}}\right)

(4.235)

where
\left(\boldsymbol{y}_{t},\boldsymbol{x}_{t}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times 1}
are
p-
dimensional vectors for
t\in\left\{1,...,n\right\}
and
\left\{\boldsymbol{A}\right\}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
are
(p\times p)
matrices of coefficients and
\boldsymbol{C}=\mathsf{diag}\left\{c_{1},...,c_{p}\right\}
is a
(p\times p)
diagonal matrix with the coefficients of persistence. The degree of persistence of the regressors is determined by the value of the unknown persistence coefficients
c_{i}
’s which are assumed to be positive constants. Moreover, the common exponent rate for the persistence coefficients take values,
\alpha=1
, which covers the local-unit-root (LUR) regressors and
\alpha\in(0,1)
the case of mildly integrated (MI) regressors as defined by
Magdalinos and Phillips, 2009
and
Kostakis et al., 2015
. When
\alpha=0
we assume the presence of stationary regressors, for a suitably restricted coefficient matrix
\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\boldsymbol{C}_{p}\right)
. A more recent application is given by
Yang et al., 2020
.

Assumption 8
.

Let
\boldsymbol{e}_{t}=\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}_{t},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}
be a
2p-
dimensional vector. The innovation sequence
\boldsymbol{e}_{t}
is a conditionally homoscedastic
martingale difference sequence
(m.d.s) such that the following conditions hold:

A1.

\mathbb{E}\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right)=\boldsymbol{0}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{t},\boldsymbol{e}_{t-1},...\right)
is an increasing sequence of
\sigma-
fields.

A2.

\mathbb{E}\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{t}\boldsymbol{e}_{t}^{\prime}|\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right)=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{ee}
, where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{ee}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
is a positive-definite covariance matrix, which has the following form:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{ee}=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{uu}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{uv}\\
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{vu}&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{vv}\end{bmatrix}>0.

such that
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{uu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{uv},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{vv}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
where
p
is the number of regressors.

A3.

The innovation to
x_{t}
is a linear process with the representation below

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{t}:=\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\boldsymbol{L})\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t}\equiv\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{j}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t-j},\ \ \ \boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t}\sim^{i.i.d}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{ee}\right)

where
\left\{\boldsymbol{\Psi}\right\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
is a sequence of absolute summable constant matrices such that
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{j}
has full rank and
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{0}=\boldsymbol{I}_{p}
with
\boldsymbol{\Psi}(1)\neq 0
, allowing for the presence of serial correlation in the innovations of the predictive regression model. Suppose that(A1)-(A3) hold, the following
FCLT
applies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor nr\rfloor}\boldsymbol{w}_{t}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor nr\rfloor}\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{u}_{t}\\
\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\end{bmatrix}\Rightarrow\ \text{BM}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{w}\right)=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{B}_{u}(r)\\
\boldsymbol{B}_{v}(r)\end{bmatrix}\equiv\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{w}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{W}(r),

(4.236)

where
\boldsymbol{w}_{t}=\left(\boldsymbol{u}_{t},\boldsymbol{v}_{t}\right)^{\prime}

and
\left\{\boldsymbol{W}(r):0\leq r\leq 1\right\}
is the standard (vector) BM, in
\mathcal{D}\left([0,1]\right)
.

Moreover, it holds that
B(r)=\omega W(r)
for some
r\in[0,1]
and
\omega:=\sum_{j=-\infty}^{\infty}\gamma_{u}(j)=C(1)^{2}\sigma^{2}
, the one-sided covariance of
v_{t}
. Moreover, by definition it holds that
W_{c}(r)=\int_{0}^{r}e^{c(r-s)}dW(s)
. Notice that, expression (
4.236
) provides an invariance principle for the partial sum process of
\boldsymbol{w}_{t}
, which implies that the partial sum of
\boldsymbol{w}_{t}
, weakly converges to the stochastic quantity
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{w}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{W}(r)
, that is, a
p-
dimensional Brownian process with covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{w}
. Within our context we employ the local-unit-root limit theory as proposed by the seminal paper of
Phillips, 1987a
, such that
\boldsymbol{x}_{[nr]}/\sqrt{n}\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)
, where
\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)
is a
p-
dimensional Gaussian process defined as below

\displaystyle\displaystyle\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)=\int_{0}^{r}e^{(r-s)\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}d\boldsymbol{W}(s),\ \ \ \ r\in(0,1).

(4.237)

that satisfies the Black-Scholes differential equation

d\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)\equiv\boldsymbol{C}_{p}\boldsymbol{K}_{c}(r)+d\boldsymbol{B}_{v}(r)
, with
\boldsymbol{K}_{c}(r)=0
, implying also that
\boldsymbol{K}_{c}(r)\equiv\sigma_{v}\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)
, where
\displaystyle\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)=\int_{0}^{r}e^{(r-s)\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}d\boldsymbol{W}(s)
. Moreover, since in this paper we consider time series nonstationarity in the form of regressors exhibiting mildly integradeness, then we also employ the asymptotic results proposed by
Phillips and Magdalinos, 2007
since the invariance principle
x_{[nr]}
requires appropriate normalization. Notice that
\boldsymbol{K}_{c}(r)
represents the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck, (OU) process, which encompasses the unit root case such that
\boldsymbol{J}_{c}(r)\equiv\boldsymbol{B}_{v}(r)
, for
\boldsymbol{C}_{p}=0
. Moreover, the assumption of a local-unit-root specification for the predictors of the model such that
\boldsymbol{x}_{t}=\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\frac{\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}{n^{\alpha}}\right)

\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{t}
and more specifically by allowing the autocorrelation coefficient to be of the form
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{n}=\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\frac{\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}{n^{\alpha}}\right)
, permits to consider the
\boldsymbol{K}_{c}(r)
Gaussian process given by (
4.237
), as a stochastic approximation.

Proof.
Expanding the expression
x_{t}z_{t}^{\prime}
, vectorizing and summing over
t\in(1,...,n)

\displaystyle x_{t}z_{t}^{\prime}=R_{n}x_{t-1}z_{t-1}^{\prime}R_{n}^{\prime}+R_{n}x_{t-1}u_{xt}^{\prime}+u_{xt}z_{t-1}^{\prime}R_{n}^{\prime}+u_{xt}u_{xt}^{\prime}

We have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\left[I_{K^{2}}-R_{zn}\otimes R_{n}\right]\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\text{vec}\left(x_{t-1}z_{t-1}^{\prime}\right)

\displaystyle=\left[I_{K}+o_{p}(1)\right]\text{vec}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}x_{t-1}u_{xt}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}u_{xt}z_{t-1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}u_{xt}u_{xt}^{\prime}\right]

\displaystyle=\text{vec}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}x_{t-1}u_{xt}^{\prime}+\Lambda^{\prime}_{xx}+\mathbb{E}\left(u_{x1}u_{x1}^{\prime}\right)\right]+o_{p}(1)

Therefore, for
\delta<\gamma
, which holds since
\gamma=1
and
\delta\in(0,1)
, then we obtain

\displaystyle\left[I_{K^{2}}-R_{zn}\otimes R_{n}\right]=-\frac{1}{n^{\delta}}\left(C_{z}\otimes I_{K}\right)\left[I_{K}+O_{p}\left(\frac{1}{n^{\gamma-\delta}}\right)\right]

(4.238)

Note for example, that

\displaystyle R_{zn}\otimes R_{n}=\left(I_{K}+\frac{C_{z}}{n^{\delta}}\right)\otimes\left(I_{K}+\frac{C}{n^{\gamma}}\right)=I_{K^{2}}+\frac{C\otimes I_{K}}{n^{\gamma}}+\frac{C_{z}\otimes I_{K}}{n^{\delta}}+\frac{C_{z}\otimes C}{n^{\gamma+\delta}}

Thus,

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n^{1+\delta}}\left(C_{z}\otimes I_{K}\right)\sum_{t=1}^{n}\text{vec}\left(x_{t-1}z_{t-1}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left(\int_{0}^{1}J_{C}dB^{\prime}_{x}+\Omega_{xx}\right)

(4.239)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}x_{t-1}z_{t-1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow-\left(\int_{0}^{1}J_{C}dB^{\prime}_{x}+\Omega_{xx}\right)C_{z}^{-1}

(4.240)

Part (L2)
. Since,
J_{c}(r)=\int_{0}^{r}e^{-c(r-s)}dB_{v}(s)
and using expression (14) in PM for the univariate predictive regression with single regressor we have that

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{t=1}^{n}z_{t-1}^{2}}{n^{1+\delta}}\overset{p}{\to}V_{c_{z}}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\sigma_{v}^{2}e^{-2rc_{z}}dr=\frac{\sigma^{2}_{v}}{2c_{z}}

(4.241)

The corresponding result for the the univariate predictive regression with multiple regressors is as below

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}z_{t-1}z_{t-1}^{\prime}\overset{p}{\to}V_{zz}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{rC_{z}}\Omega_{xx}e^{rC_{z}}dr

(4.242)

Recall that by definition the functional
\underline{J}_{c}(r)=\int_{0}^{r}e^{(r-s)C}d\underline{B}(s)
, is considered to be a vector diffusion process and satisfies the stochastic differential equation system

\displaystyle d\underline{J}_{c}(r)=C\underline{J}_{c}(r)dr+d\underline{B}(r),\ \ \underline{J}_{c}(0)=0

(4.243)

Therefore, using the initial condition as well we can also write

\displaystyle\underline{J}_{c}(r)=\underline{B}(r)+C\int_{0}^{r}e^{(r-s)C}\underline{B}(s)ds

(4.244)

Since
\underline{J}_{c}(r)
is a Gaussian process and for fixed
r
then it can be easily proved that the finite dimensional distribution (see e.g.,
Phillips, 1988
)

\displaystyle\underline{J}_{c}(r)\Rightarrow N(0,Q)\ \ \text{where}\ Q=\int_{0}^{r}e^{(r-s)C}\Omega e^{(r-s)C^{\prime}}ds

(4.245)

where
\Omega
the
p\times p
covariance matrix of
\underline{B}(r)
the
p-
dimensional Brownian motion.

Part (L3)
. The weakly convergence result to a mixed Gaussian distribution below

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\delta}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(z_{t-1}\otimes u_{t}\right)\Rightarrow N\left(0,V_{zz}\otimes\Sigma_{uu}\right)

(4.246)

shows that the limit distribution of
T^{-(1+\delta)/2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(z_{t-1}\otimes u_{t}\right)
is Gaussian with mean zero and covariance matrix equal to the probability limit of
T^{-(1+\delta)/2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(z_{t-1}\otimes u_{t}\right)
, which is equal to
V_{zz}\otimes\Sigma_{uu}
, where
V_{zz}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{rC_{z}}\Omega_{vv}e^{rC_{z}}dr
. Similarly the limit distribution of
T^{-(1+\delta)/2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(x_{t-1}\otimes u_{t}\right)\Rightarrow N\left(0,V_{xx}\otimes\Sigma_{uu}\right)
, where
V_{xx}:=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{rC}\Omega_{vv}e^{rC}dr
, is proved in
Lemma 3.3 of
Magdalinos and Phillips, 2009
.

We prove the weakly convergence result given above for the univariate predictive regression (applied to both the case of single versus multiple regressors). See also case 2 of Lemma B3 in Appendix of KMS, where the result for the multivariate predictive regression is proved. We consider the partial sum process
S_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{t}z_{j}
where
z_{j}
is the IVX instrument. Therefore, we obtain
\Delta S_{t-1}\equiv S_{t-1}-S_{t-2}=z_{t-1}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}z_{t-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}\Delta S_{t-1}=\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}\left(S_{t-1}-S_{t-2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-1}x_{t-1}-\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-2}x_{t-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\left\{\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-1}x_{t-1}-\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-2}x_{t-2}\right]-\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-2}\Delta x_{t-1}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\left(S_{T-1}x_{T-1}-S_{0}x_{0}\right)-\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-2}\Delta x_{t-1}

The first term of the above expression is considered to be asymptotically negligible, i.e.,
o_{p}(1)
. Thus, we consider the asymptotic behaviour of the second term by expanding the expression further. We have that
\displaystyle\Delta x_{t}=\frac{C}{T}x_{t-1}+v_{t}
, implying

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-2}\Delta x_{t-1}=\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-2}v_{t-1}+\frac{C}{T^{2+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-2}x_{t-2}

(4.247)

Therefore, the above gives the following

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}z_{t-1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\left\{\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-2}v_{t-1}+\frac{C}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-2}x_{t-2}\right\}=-\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\left\{\sum_{t=1}^{T}S_{t-2}\left(v_{t-1}+\frac{C}{T}x_{t-2}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\left\{\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{j=1}^{t-2}z_{j}\left(v_{t-1}+\frac{C}{T}x_{t-2}\right)\right\}

We prove the limiting behaviour of the above terms using the convergence of the martingale expression (16) in PM and the fact that
x_{t}
is a local to unity process.

Then, the following weakly convergence of (
4.248
) and (
4.249
) follows

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sum_{j=1}^{t-2}z_{j}v_{t-1}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\int_{0}^{1}\underline{J}_{c}(r)dB_{v}C_{z}^{-1}

(4.248)

\displaystyle\frac{C}{n^{2+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{t-2}z_{j}x_{t-2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\Omega_{vv}C_{z}^{-1}

(4.249)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{1+\delta}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}z_{t-1}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow-\left(\int_{0}^{1}\underline{J}_{c}(r)dB_{v}+\Omega_{vv}\right)C^{-1}_{z}

(4.250)

4.7.5.
Predictive Regression with Multiple Regressors

Consider the predictive regression model such as

\displaystyle y_{t}

\displaystyle=\beta_{0}+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}_{1}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}+u_{t},

(4.251)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{R}_{T}\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{t}

(4.252)

where
y_{t}
is a scalar vector and
\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}
is a
k-
dimensional vector of regressors. Denote with
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}=\big[u_{t},\boldsymbol{v}_{t}^{\prime}\big]^{\prime}
.

Then, the predictive regression above includes a model intercept and furthermore we do not demean the random variables in order to remove the model intercept. In addition, we assume that
\boldsymbol{R}_{T}\neq\boldsymbol{I}_{T}
which would correspond to the integrated regressor case.

Fully Modified OLS
Under the aforementioned conditions the functional law

\displaystyle\displaystyle T^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor Tr\rfloor}\overset{d}{\to}\boldsymbol{B}(r)\equiv BM(\Omega)

holds for partial sums of
\boldsymbol{\eta}_{t}
. Define the partition
\boldsymbol{B}=\big(B_{u},\boldsymbol{B}_{v}^{\prime}\big)^{\prime}
.

Proposition 12
.

Under Assumptions it holds that

\displaystyle T\left(\widehat{A}^{+}-A\right)\overset{d}{\to}\left(\int_{0}^{1}d\boldsymbol{B}_{u.v}\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}_{v}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{1}\boldsymbol{B}_{v}\boldsymbol{B}^{\prime}_{v}\right)

(4.253)

When
\Omega_{uu.v}
has full rank, the rate of convergence of the FM-OLS estimator is determined by the rates of weak convergence of the sample covariances and the rate of nonparametric estimation of
\Omega
and
\Gamma^{+}
does not apply any role.
In particular, the OLS estimator
\widehat{A}^{+}=Y^{\prime}X\left(X^{\prime}X\right)^{-1}
and employs corrections for endogeneity in the regressor
x_{t}
, leading to the transformed dependent variable

\displaystyle\widehat{y}_{t}^{+}=y_{t}-\widehat{\Omega}_{0x}\widehat{\Omega}_{xx}^{-1}\big(x_{t}-x_{t-1}\big)

(4.254)

and a bias correction term involving

\displaystyle\widehat{\Delta}_{0x}^{+}=\widehat{\Delta}_{0x}-\widehat{\Omega}_{0x}\widehat{\Omega}_{xx}^{-1}\widehat{\Delta}_{xx}

(4.255)

which is constructed in the usual way using consistent nonparametric estimators of submatrices of the long run and one sided long run quantities
\Omega
and
\Gamma^{+}
. In particular, the FM-OLS estimators removes asymptotic bias and increases efficiency by correcting both the long run serial correlation in
u_{t}
and endogeneity in
x_{t}
causes by the long run correlation between
u_{0t}
and
u_{xt}
.

Example 24

(Mildly Explosive Autoregression)
.

Consider the martingale difference property of
\left\{\hat{\epsilon}_{n,t}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}\left[\epsilon_{1t}\right]:t\geq 1\right\}
which yields that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{t=1}^{k_{n}}a_{n,t}\big(\epsilon_{1t}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}\left[\epsilon_{1t}\right]\big)^{2}\right\rVert\leq\Delta_{n}^{2}\sum_{t=1}^{k_{n}}a_{n,t}^{2}\to 0.

(4.256)

by some choice of
(\Delta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Furthermore, the fact that
(y_{t})
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}-
adapted implies that, for any fixed integer
M>0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{k_{n}}a_{n,t}y_{t}=\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\sum_{t=1}^{k_{n}}a_{n,t}\big(\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{t-m}}\left[y_{t}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{t-m-1}}\left[y_{t}\right]\big)+\sum_{t=1}^{k_{n}}a_{n,t}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{t-M}}[y_{t}]

(4.257)

Thus, for each
m
, it holds that

\displaystyle\epsilon_{t}^{(m)}:=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{t-m}}[y_{t}]-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{t-m-1}}[y_{t}]

(4.258)

is a
\mathcal{F}_{t-m}-
martingale difference process that inherits the uniform integrability property from
y_{t}
, and so,
\epsilon_{t}^{(m)}
satisfies the required conditions. Then, applying the triangle inequality we obtain the following

\displaystyle\left\lVert\sum_{t=1}^{n}a_{n,t}y_{t}\right\rVert_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}\left\lVert\sum_{t=1}^{k_{n}}a_{n,t}\epsilon_{t}^{(m)}\right\rVert_{1}+\sum_{t=1}^{k_{n}}\left|a_{n,t}\right|\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{t-M}}[y_{t}]\right\rVert_{1}

(4.259)

\displaystyle\leq M\times\underset{0\leq m<M}{\mathsf{max}}\left\lVert\sum_{t=1}^{k_{n}}a_{n,t}\epsilon_{t}^{(m)}\right\rVert_{1}+\zeta\underset{n\in\mathbb{N}}{\mathsf{sup}}\sum_{t=1}^{k_{n}}\left|a_{n,t}\right|\psi_{M}.

(4.260)

Let
\delta>0
be arbitray. Since
C:=\zeta\mathsf{sup}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{t=1}^{k_{n}}\left|a_{n,t}\right|<\infty
and
\psi_{M}\to\infty
, there exists
M_{0}(\delta)\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\psi_{M}\leq\delta/(2C)
.

Example 25
.

Consider the case in which all regressors,
X_{t}
, is a
p\times 1
vector of
I(1)
variables such that

\displaystyle X_{t}=X_{t-1}+\eta_{t},\ \ 1\leq t\leq n

(4.261)

where
X_{0}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(1)
and
\underset{1\leq t\leq n}{\mathsf{max}}\ \mathbb{E}\left(\left\lVert\eta_{t}\right\rVert^{q}\right)\leq C<\infty
for some
q>8
.

Furthermore,
\left\{u_{t}\right\}_{t=1}^{n}
is independent of
\left\{(X_{t},Z_{t})\right\}_{t=1}^{n}
and
\left\{\big(\left(u_{t},\eta_{t}\right),\mathcal{F}_{n,t-1}\big)\right\}_{2\leq t\leq n}
and forms a martingale difference sequence with
\sigma_{v}^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left(v_{t}^{2}\right)<\infty
, such that

\displaystyle\underset{2\leq t\leq 2}{\mathsf{sup}}\left|\mathbb{E}\left(v_{t}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{n,t-1}\right)-\sigma_{v}^{2}\right|\to 0

(4.262)

Denote with

\displaystyle B_{n,\eta}(r)\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor nr\rfloor}\eta_{t}.

(4.263)

There exists a vector Brownian motion
B_{\eta}
such that
B_{n,\eta}(r)\Rightarrow B_{\eta}(r)
, on
D[0,1]^{d}
as
n\to\infty
, where
D[0,1]^{d}
is the space of cadlag functions on
[0,1]^{d}
equipped with Skorohod topology, and
B_{\eta}
is a
d-
dimensional multivariate Brownian motion with a finite positive definite covariance matrix
\Sigma_{\eta}
such that

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\eta}=\underset{n\to\infty}{\mathsf{lim}}\text{Var}\left(n^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\eta_{t}\right)

(4.264)

The martingale difference condition is required to employ the generalized martingale central limit theorem when we derive the limiting distribution of our proposed test statistic under the null hypothesis. Furthermore, taking the weak convergence result in (
4.263
) as an assumption is commonly done in the econometrics literature. The conditions for the multivariate functional central limit theorem for partial sums of weakly dependent random vectors can be found
Wooldridge and White, 1988
. Thus, by applying the continuous mapping theorem it follows that

\displaystyle\underset{0\leq r\leq 1}{\mathsf{sup}}\left|B_{n,\eta}\right|\overset{d}{\to}\underset{0\leq r\leq 1}{\mathsf{sup}}\left|B_{\eta}\right|

(4.265)

Since it is bounded in probability, that is,
\underset{0\leq r\leq 1}{\mathsf{sup}}\left|B_{\eta}(r)\right|=\mathcal{O}_{p}(1)
, hence we have that

\displaystyle\underset{1\leq t\leq n}{\mathsf{max}}\left|X_{t}\right|=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\sqrt{n}\right),

(4.266)

4.7.6.
Testing for Structural Break in Predictive Regression

In this section, we present the framework of
Georgiev et al., 2018
. The approach of the particular paper is to consider between two important test statistics which are commonly used in detecting structural change, that is, the sup Wald test of
Andrews, 1993
and the Cramer-von-Mises type statistics of
Nyblom, 1989
. On the one hand the sup Wald test proposed by
Andrews, 1993
is motivated by alternatives where the parameters display a small number of breaks at deterministic points in the sample, while under the alternative hypothesis of the Cramer-von-Mises test statistic the coefficients of the model are random and slowly evolve through time. Using a fixed regressor wild bootstrap procedure their framework allows for both conditional and unconditional heteroscedasticity in the data.

Consider the time-varying predictive regression model

\displaystyle y_{t}=\alpha_{t}+\beta_{t}x_{t-1}+\epsilon_{yt},\ \ t=1,...,T.

(4.267)

where
\epsilon_{yt}
is a mean zero innovation process and
x_{t}
is an observed process, generated by

\displaystyle x_{t}

\displaystyle=\mu+s_{xt},\ \ s_{xt}=\rho_{x}s_{xt-1}+\epsilon_{xt}

(4.268)

where
\rho_{x}:=\left(1-\frac{c_{x}}{T}\right)
, with
c_{x}\leq 0
which allows to consider two types of persistence properties for the regressors of the model, that is, (i) strongly persistence unit root, and (ii) local-to-unit root.

Remark 22
.

Note that (
4.267
) provides a time-varying representation of the predictive regression model, which allows both the model intercept and the slope coefficients to vary over time. In particular, the parameters are expressed as

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}:=\alpha+as_{\alpha t},\ \ \ \beta_{t}:=\beta+bs_{\beta t}

(4.269)

For instance, in the context of a time-invariant model, that is,
\alpha_{t}=\alpha
and
\beta_{t}=\beta
. Therefore, with near-unit root predictors then the framework of Cavanagh et al (1995) applies. Moreover, when
\beta
is fixed, then the predictive regression model can be interpreted as a co-integrating regression because
y_{t}
is a near-unit root process. The main focus of the proposed framework is on testing the null hypotheses that the intercept and the slope parameters are constant over time against the alternative that they vary over time through the sequences of associated time-varying coefficients,
s_{\alpha t}
and
s_{\beta t}
. In particular, this can be done by testing the restrictions that
a=0
and
b=0
.

Stochastic Coefficient Variation

The assumption of time-varying parameters in the predictive regression model implies that the time-varying components of the model parameters, that is,
s_{\alpha t}
and
s_{\beta t}
follow (near-) unit root processes expressed as below

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}s_{\alpha t}\\
s_{\beta t}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\rho_{\alpha}&0\\
0&\rho_{\beta}\\
\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}s_{\alpha t-1}\\
s_{\beta t-1}\end{bmatrix}+\begin{bmatrix}\epsilon_{\alpha t}\\
\epsilon_{\beta t}\end{bmatrix}

(4.270)

where the autocorrelation coefficient is expressed as below

\displaystyle\rho_{\alpha}:=\left(1-\frac{c_{\alpha}}{T}\right),\ \ \rho_{\beta}:=\left(1-\frac{c_{\beta}}{T}\right),\ \ \text{with}\ \ c_{\alpha}\geq 0\ \ \text{and}\ \ c_{\beta}\geq 0.

(4.271)

which are unit-root or local-to-unit root autoregressive processes.

The non-stochastic coefficient variation mechanism includes the testing framework proposed by
Andrews, 1993
. Under the assumption of one-time breakpoint, then
s_{\alpha t}
and
s_{\beta t}
are modelled as below

\displaystyle s_{\alpha t}=s_{\beta t}=D_{t}\left(\lfloor\pi_{0}T\rfloor\right)

(4.272)

where we define
D_{t}\left(\lfloor\pi_{0}T\rfloor\right):=\mathbf{1}\left(t\leq\lfloor\pi T\rfloor\right)
with
\lfloor\pi T\rfloor
denoting a generic shift point with associated break fraction
\pi
. We take the true shift fraction
\pi_{0}\in\Pi
, where
\Pi=[\pi_{1},\pi_{2}]
with
0<\pi_{1}<\pi_{2}<1
.

Consider the testing hypothesis given by

\displaystyle H_{0}:a\neq 0\ \ \text{versus}\ \ \ b\neq 0.

(4.273)

Moreover, consider the fitted regression model

\displaystyle y_{t}=\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\beta}x_{t-1}+\hat{\beta}_{0}\Delta x_{t}+\hat{e}_{t},\ \ \ t=1,...,T

(4.274)

Let
\hat{\sigma}^{2}:=T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\hat{e}_{t}^{2}
, with
\hat{e}_{t}^{2}
the OLS residual from the fitted regression given by (
4.274
). Moreover, we denote with
\underline{X}=[1\ x_{t-1}]^{\prime}
.

Then, the LM statistic is constructed as

\displaystyle LM:=\frac{1}{T\hat{\sigma}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{T}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{j}\underline{X}_{t}\hat{e}_{t}\right)\left(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\underline{X}_{t}\underline{X}_{t}^{\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{j}\underline{X}_{t}\hat{e}_{t}\right)

(4.275)

Note that the corresponding single parameter LM statistics are given by

\displaystyle LM_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{T^{2}\hat{\sigma}^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{T}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{j}\hat{e}_{t}\right)^{2}

(4.276)

\displaystyle LM_{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\left(T^{2}\hat{\sigma}^{2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}^{2}\right)}\sum_{j=1}^{T}\left(\sum_{t=1}^{j}x_{t-1}\hat{e}_{t}\right)^{2}

(4.277)

where LM
1
corresponds to the test statistic relating the the intercept alone and LM
2
to the slope coefficient alone.

Theorem 12
.

Consider the model in (1)-(3) and let Assumption 2 hold. Under the null hypothesis and under the same local alternatives , the following converge jointly as
T\to\infty
, in the sense of weak convergence of random measures on
\mathbb{R}:

\displaystyle LM_{x}\big|x\to_{w}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbf{J}^{\prime}(r)\left[\mathbf{V}(1)\right]^{-1}\mathbf{J}^{\prime}(r)dr\bigg|B_{1}

(4.278)

and

\displaystyle LM^{*}_{x}\big|x,y\to_{w}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbf{J}_{0}^{\prime}(r)\left[\mathbf{V}(1)\right]^{-1}\mathbf{J}_{0}^{\prime}(r)dr\bigg|B_{1}

(4.279)

where
\mathbf{J}_{0}(r):=\int_{0}^{r}\mathbf{A}(s)dY(s),r\in[0,1]
.

Similarly, in the sense of weak convergence of random measures on
\mathbb{R}
, the following convergence jointly as
T\to\infty:

\displaystyle\text{supF}_{x}\big|x\to_{w}\text{sup}_{r\in\Lambda}\bigg\{\mathbf{J}^{\prime}(r)\bigg[\mathbf{V}(r)-\mathbf{V}(r)\mathbf{V}(1)^{-1}\mathbf{V}(r)\bigg]^{-1}\mathbf{J}(r)\bigg\}\bigg|B_{1}

(4.280)

and

\displaystyle\text{supF}^{*}_{x}\big|x,y\to_{w}\text{sup}_{r\in\Lambda}\bigg\{\mathbf{J}_{0}^{\prime}(r)\bigg[\mathbf{V}(r)-\mathbf{V}(r)\mathbf{V}(1)^{-1}\mathbf{V}(r)\bigg]^{-1}\mathbf{J}_{0}(r)\bigg\}\bigg|B_{1}:=\mathcal{J}_{0}\big|B_{1}.

(4.281)

Supplementary Examples

In this section we provide additional examples related to testing for parameter instability in predictive regression models. Further derivations can be found in the studies of
Katsouris, 2023b
;
Katsouris, 2023d
;
Katsouris, 2023e
. First, consider the case in which the predictive regression model includes a model intercept but is assumed to be stable before conducting any statistical inference on the existence of a structural break on the slopes of the model.

Example 26
.

Consider the following predictive regression model

\displaystyle y_{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{\prime}x_{t-1}I\{t\leq k\}+\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{\prime}x_{t-1}I\{t>k\}+u_{t}

(4.282)

where
y_{t}
is a scalar (i.e., univariate predictant) and
x_{t}
is
p-
dimensional vector of predictors (i.e., multiple predictors). Moreover,
k=\lfloor T\pi\rfloor
denotes the unknown break-point location in the sample such that
\pi\in(0,1)
. The predictors of the predictive regression model are generated as a LUR process given by

\displaystyle x_{t}=\left(\mathbf{I}_{p}-\frac{\mathbf{C}}{T^{\gamma_{x}}}\right)x_{t-1}+v_{t}

(4.283)

where
C>0
, i.e.,
c_{i}>0
for all
i\in\left\{1,...,p\right\}
and
\gamma_{x}\in(0,1)
(MI). We are interested to test:

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}:\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}=\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\ \ \ \text{against}\ \ \ \mathbb{H}_{1}:\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}\neq\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2},

(4.284)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{T}^{OLS}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{u}^{2}}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}\right)^{\prime}\left[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\left(\boldsymbol{X}^{*\prime}\boldsymbol{X}^{*}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}\right]^{-1}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}\right)

(4.285)

Therefore, when a model intercept is included in the model and is assumed to remain stable, then we shall consider the demeaned versions of the variables and reformulated the regression specification. The model can be written as:

\displaystyle y_{t}^{*}=\beta_{1}x_{t-1}^{*}I\{t\leq k\}+\beta_{2}x_{t-1}^{*}I\{t>k\}+u_{t}^{*}\ \ \ \ \ \ \

(4.286)

such that the above predictive regression model considers the demeaned variables as below:

\displaystyle y_{t}^{*}=y_{t}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}y_{t},\ x_{1t-1}^{*}=x_{1t-1}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1},\ x_{2t-1}^{*}=x_{2t-1}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{2t-1},\ u_{t}^{*}=u_{t}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}u_{t}.

where
x_{1t-1}:=x_{t-1}I\{t\leq k\}
and
x_{2t-1}:=x_{t-1}I\{t>k\}
. In matrix notation we use
X_{1}^{*}=\left(X_{1}-\bar{X}_{1}\right)
and
X_{2}^{*}=\left(X_{2}-\bar{X}_{2}\right)
where
\bar{X}_{1}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}
and
\bar{X}_{2}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{2t-1}
.
Also,
u_{t}^{*}
denotes the average-corrected error sequence of the predictive regression model given by (
4.316
).

Remark 23
.

Notice that while the regressors
\boldsymbol{X}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{2}
are orthogonal regressors, the corresponding regressors
\boldsymbol{X}_{1}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{2}^{*}
which occur after considering the demeaned versions of their counterparts are not orthogonal. For this reason one has to be careful when constructing the estimators for the parameters
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}
with respect to the regressors
\boldsymbol{X}_{1}^{*}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{2}^{*}
, as well as the corresponding covariance matrix.

More specifically, while asymptotically the conventional OLS estimators can be used without further modifications in finite-samples the standard formula will not hold. Similarly the covariance matrix can be simplified only asymptotically, which implies having off-diagonal terms converging in probability to zero, however this result does not hold in finite samples. In practise, we have the following specification i.e., a predictive regression model with multiple regressors written as below

\displaystyle y_{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha+\beta_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}_{1t-1}+\boldsymbol{x}_{2t-1}\beta_{2}^{\prime}+u_{t}

\displaystyle\bar{y}_{T}

\displaystyle=\alpha+\beta_{1}^{\prime}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1T}+\beta_{2}^{\prime}\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2T}+\bar{u}_{T}

\displaystyle\left(y_{t}-\bar{y}_{T}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta_{1}^{\prime}\left(\boldsymbol{x}_{1t-1}-\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1T}\right)+\beta_{2}^{\prime}\left(\boldsymbol{x}_{2t-1}-\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{2T}\right)+\left(u_{t}-\bar{u}_{T}\right)

And by simplifying the notation we write

\displaystyle y_{t}^{*}=\beta_{1}x_{1t-1}^{*}+\beta_{2}x_{2t-1}^{*}+u_{t}^{*}\ \ \ \ \ \ \

where
x_{1t-1}:=x_{t-1}I\{t\leq k\}
and
x_{2t-1}:=x_{t-1}I\{t>k\}
.

Remark 24
.

Specifically, continuing the discussion from Remark 1, the expressions
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}=\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{1}^{*\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{1}^{\prime}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{1}^{*\prime}u^{*}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}=\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{2}^{*\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{2}^{\prime}\right)\boldsymbol{X}_{2}^{*\prime}u^{*}
will not hold in finite samples due to the fact that
\boldsymbol{X}_{1}^{*\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{1}^{\prime}\neq 0
in finite samples. Moreover since we operate within the predictive regression model where regressors are generated as LUR processes, we have to note that these results hold for the mildly integrated case. For instance, in the case of near stationary regressors these effects are not contributing even after demeaning, which just implies that are still valid asymptotically for the near stationary case. On the other hand, for the case of LUR regressors, i.e.,
\gamma_{x}=1
, then the demeaning effect is contributing to finite samples and therefore we need to check the terms for the parameter estimators and the corresponding covariance matrix.

Therefore, we have the following expression

\displaystyle T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{1t-1}x_{1t-1}^{*\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{1t-1}u^{*}_{t}\right)+o_{p}(1).

(4.287)

Consider the first term of (
4.287
)

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{1t-1}x_{1t-1}^{*\prime}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(x_{1t-1}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}\right)\left(x_{1t-1}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}\right)^{\prime}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}x_{1t-1}^{\prime}-T\bar{x}_{1t-1}\bar{x}_{1t-1}^{\prime}

Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{1t-1}x_{1t-1}^{*\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}x_{1t-1}^{\prime}-\frac{T}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\left(\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}\right)\left(\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}\right)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}x_{1t-1}^{\prime}-\left(\frac{\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}}{T^{\frac{\gamma_{x}}{2}+1}}\right)\left(\frac{\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}}{T^{\frac{\gamma_{x}}{2}+1}}\right)^{\prime}

Since as
T\to\infty
,
\frac{\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}}{T^{\frac{\gamma_{x}}{2}+1}}\to_{p}0
for
\gamma_{x}\in(0,1)
, then we obtain that

\displaystyle\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{1t-1}x_{1t-1}^{*\prime}\right\}

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}x_{1t-1}^{\prime}\right\}=\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}x_{t-1}^{\prime}I\left\{t\leq k\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor T\pi\rfloor}x_{t-1}x_{t-1}^{\prime}\right\}\Rightarrow\pi\mathbf{V}_{c}

Consider the second term of (
4.287
)

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{1t-1}u^{*}_{t}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(x_{1t-1}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}\right)\left(u_{t}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}u_{t}\right)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}u_{t}-\bar{u}_{t}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}-\bar{x}_{t-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(u_{t}-\bar{u}_{t}\right)

Since the last term above is zero, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{1t-1}u^{*}_{t}=\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}u_{t}-\bar{u}_{T}\frac{\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}

(4.288)

In other words,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{1t-1}u^{*}_{t}=\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}u_{t}+o_{p}(1).

Proof.

Notice that
\bar{u}_{T}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}u_{t}
, where
u_{t}\sim_{\textit{i.i.d}}(0,\sigma^{2})
which implies that
\bar{u}_{T}=\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right)
. Another way to think about it is that since
T^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}u_{t}\overset{p}{\to}\mathbb{E}\left(u_{t}\right)
then
T^{-3/2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}u_{t}=o_{p}(1)
. Notice that for the LUR process,
x_{t}=\rho x_{t-1}+u_{t}
, we have that
u_{t}
is a martingale difference sequence with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma\left(u_{t},u_{t-1},...\right)
satisfying
\mathbb{E}\left(u_{t}^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right)=\sigma^{2}>0
for all
t
, and
\left(u_{t}^{2}\right)_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a uniformly integrable sequence. Therefore, by expanding the autoregressive model, where
\rho_{T}=\left(1-\frac{c}{T}^{\gamma_{x}}\right)
, for some
c>0
and
\gamma_{x}\in(0,1)
, we obtain the following expression:

\displaystyle\left(1-\rho_{T}\right)\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}u_{t}-\left(x_{T}-x_{0}\right)

Notice that
x_{T}
dominates (faster asymptotic convergence) the term
\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}
in the mildly integrated case, i.e.,
\gamma_{x}\in(0,1)
since the quantity
x_{T}
is
\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(T^{-\frac{\gamma_{x}}{2}}\right)
while the quantity
\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}
is
o_{p}(1)
. We can see the first result by considering the order of convergence of the term
\mathbb{E}\left[x_{T}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{t}\rho_{T}^{t-j}x_{t-j}
which is
\mathcal{O}_{p}\left(T^{\frac{\gamma_{x}}{2}}\right)
. Moreover, it also holds that
\sum_{t=1}^{T}u_{t}=o_{p}(1)
. Thus, the order of convergence is

\displaystyle\bar{u}_{T}\frac{\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\overset{p}{\to}\mathcal{O}_{p}(1)\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\frac{\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}}{T^{\frac{\gamma_{x}}{2}}}=\mathcal{O}_{p}(1)o_{p}(1)=o_{p}(1)

(4.289)

∎

Recall that if for some sequence
M_{n}
it holds that
m_{n}=\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{n})
then the sequence
nM_{n}=\mathcal{O}(1)
is a bounded sequence. Since as
T\to\infty
,
\bar{u}_{t}\to 0
while
T^{-\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}
is bounded, then we have that

\displaystyle\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{1t-1}u^{*}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{1t-1}u_{t}\right\}=\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}u_{t}I\left\{t\leq k\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{\lfloor T\pi\rfloor}x_{t-1}u_{t}\right\}\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)

Therefore, we obtain the convergence result below:

\displaystyle T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\right)\Rightarrow\bigg(\pi\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)^{-1}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)=\frac{1}{\pi}\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)

(4.290)

We also have the following expression

\displaystyle T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{2t-1}x_{2t-1}^{*\prime}\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{2t-1}u^{*}_{t}\right)+o_{p}(1).

(4.291)

Consider the second term of (
4.291
)

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{2t-1}u^{*}_{t}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(x_{2t-1}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{2t-1}\right)\left(u_{t}-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}u_{t}\right)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{2t-1}u_{t}-\bar{u}_{t}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{2t-1}-\bar{x}_{t-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(u_{t}-\bar{u}_{t}\right)

Since the last term above is zero, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{2t-1}u^{*}_{t}=\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{2t-1}u_{t}-\bar{u}_{T}\frac{\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{2t-1}}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}

(4.292)

In other words,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{2t-1}u^{*}_{t}=\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{2t-1}u_{t}+o_{p}(1).

and the proof of the above holds due to similar arguments as in the case of
\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{1t-1}u^{*}_{t}
. Using similar arguments we can show that

\displaystyle\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{2t-1}x_{2t-1}^{*\prime}\right\}

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{2t-1}x_{2t-1}^{\prime}\right\}=\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}x_{t-1}^{\prime}I\left\{t>k\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\sum_{t=\lfloor T\pi\rfloor+1}^{T}x_{t-1}x_{t-1}^{\prime}\right\}\Rightarrow(1-\pi)\mathbf{V}_{c}

(4.293)

and

\displaystyle\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x^{*}_{2t-1}u^{*}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\equiv\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{2t-1}u_{t}\right\}=\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}x_{t-1}u_{t}I\left\{t>k\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\underset{T\to\infty}{\text{plim}}\left\{\frac{1}{T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}}\sum_{t=\lfloor T\pi\rfloor+1}^{T}x_{t-1}u_{t}\right\}\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,(1-\pi)\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)

Therefore, we obtain the convergence result below:

\displaystyle T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}-\boldsymbol{\beta}^{0}\right)\Rightarrow\bigg((1-\pi)\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)^{-1}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,(1-\pi)\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)\equiv\frac{1}{1-\pi}\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,(1-\pi)\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)

(4.294)

Now, using expressions (
4.290
) and (
4.294
) we obtain the following simplified expression

\displaystyle T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left\{\frac{1}{\pi}\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)-\frac{1}{1-\pi}\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,(1-\pi)\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)-\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)}{\pi(1-\pi)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)-\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)}{\pi(1-\pi)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}}{\pi(1-\pi)}\left[\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)-\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}}{\pi(1-\pi)}\left[\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)-\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)\right]\right\}

(4.295)

where
\gamma_{x}\in(0,1)
and
\pi
the unknown break fraction. We construct the OLS Wald statistic for testing the null hypothesis
H_{0}:\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\boldsymbol{\beta}=0
, which is equivalent to the null of no parameter instability,
such that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}=\left[\mathbf{I}_{p}\ -\mathbf{I}_{p}\right]
and
\boldsymbol{\beta}=\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\right)
, where
\boldsymbol{\beta}
is the model parameter and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}=\left(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}\right)
the OLS estimator.

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{T}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}_{u}^{2}}\left[T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}\right)\right]^{\prime}\left[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}^{*\prime}\boldsymbol{X}^{*}}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}\right]^{-1}\left[T^{\frac{1+\gamma_{x}}{2}}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}\right)\right]

(4.296)

where

\displaystyle\left[\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{X}^{*\prime}\boldsymbol{X}^{*}}{T^{1+\gamma_{x}}}\right)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{R}}^{\prime}\right]\Rightarrow\left[\bigg(\pi\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)^{-1}+\bigg((1-\pi)\mathbf{V}_{c}\bigg)^{-1}\right]=\left[\frac{1}{\pi}\mathbf{V}_{c}+\frac{1}{1-\pi}\mathbf{V}_{c}\right]=\frac{\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1}}{\pi(1-\pi)}

Therefore, based on expressions (
26
), (
4.296
) and (
26
) the simplified asymptotic result for the OLS-Wald statistic is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{T}(\pi)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{1}{\sigma_{u}^{2}}\left\{\frac{\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}}{\pi(1-\pi)}\left[\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)-\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)\right]\right\}^{\prime}\left[\frac{\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1}}{\pi(1-\pi)}\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \times\left\{\frac{\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}}{\pi(1-\pi)}\left[\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)-\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma_{u}^{2}}\frac{1}{\pi(1-\pi)}\left[\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)-\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)\right]^{\prime}\left(\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}\right)^{\prime}\mathbf{V}_{c}\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \times\left[\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)-\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)\right]

(4.297)

Notice that since

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}\right)^{\prime}\mathbf{V}_{c}\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}=\left(\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}\right)^{\prime}\mathbf{V}_{c}^{1/2}\left(\mathbf{V}_{c}^{1/2}\right)^{\prime}\mathbf{V}_{c}^{-1/2}=\mathbf{I}_{p}

(4.298)

Then the limit result for the Wald OLS statistic simplifies to the following expression

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{T}(\pi)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{1}{\sigma_{u}^{2}}\frac{1}{\pi(1-\pi)}\left[\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)-\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)\right]^{\prime}\left[\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\pi\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)-\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\sigma_{u}^{2}\otimes\mathbf{I}_{p}\bigg)\right]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{\bigg[\mathbf{W}_{p}(\pi)-\pi\mathbf{W}_{p}(1)\bigg]^{\prime}\bigg[\mathbf{W}_{p}(\pi)-\pi\mathbf{W}_{p}(1)\bigg]}{\pi(1-\pi)}:=\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p}(\pi)^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p}(\pi)}{\pi(1-\pi)}

where
\mathbf{W}_{p}(\pi)
is a Brownian motion with covariance matrix
\sigma_{u}^{2}
. In summary, we have proved that the limiting distribution of the OLS-Wald statistic for testing the null hypothesis of parameter constancy for the parameters of the predictive regression model when: (i) a model intercept is included but is assumed to remain stable through out the sample and (ii) the regressors are modelled as mildly integrated regressors,it weakly convergence to a normalized Brownian Bridge, i.e., the standard NBB limit result of Andrews is preserved. Therefore, similarly the corresponding limiting distribution of the sup OLS-Wald statistic is the sup of a NBB process. In other words, it follows that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}^{OLS}(\pi)\Rightarrow\underset{\pi\in[\pi_{1},\pi_{2}]}{\text{sup}}\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p}(\pi)^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p}(\pi)}{\pi(1-\pi)},

(4.299)

where
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p}(.)
is a
p-
dimensional standard Brownian bridge, such that
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p}(.):=\left[\mathbf{W}_{p}(.)-\pi\mathbf{W}_{p}(1)\right]
.

Notice that in the case of a fixed break-point, say
\pi\equiv\pi_{0}
, then since
\mathbf{W}_{p}(\pi_{0})-\pi_{0}\mathbf{W}_{p}(1)\equiv\mathcal{N}\big(0,\pi_{0}(1-\pi_{0})\big)
, then it holds that
\displaystyle\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p}(\pi_{0})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{BB}}_{p}(\pi_{0})}{\pi_{0}(1-\pi_{0})}\equiv\sum_{j=1}^{p}\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{2}=\chi^{2}(p)
which shows that the OLS-Wald is free of any nuisance parameters.

4.7.7.
Main Aspects on Predictive Regressions

A research question of interest is whether future values of one series
\left\{Y_{t}\right\}
can be predicted from lagged values of another series
\left\{X_{t}\right\}
, where the two series obey the following model:

\displaystyle Y_{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha+\beta X_{t-1}+u_{t},

(4.300)

\displaystyle X_{t}

\displaystyle=\mu+\rho X_{t-1}+v_{t},

(4.301)

It is well known that the OLS estimate of
\beta
is biased when the errors
\left\{u_{t},v_{t}\right\}
are correlated, with the amount of bias increasing as
\rho
get closer to the unit boundary. There is currently no known theoretical justification that inference (e.g., a t-test) based on the bias-corrected OLS estimate will have improved size properties relative to the test based on the uncorrected OLS estimate. In practise, there are several studies in the literature that provide examples where even the use of the exact bias correction does not result in accurate finite-sample inference because the resulting t-statistic can still be very far from normal. Therefore, from an inferential point of view, issues such as the bias of point estimates may be irrelevant in small samples, and thus it is also crucial to examine the likelihood. At first glance, it may seem surprising that a likelihood ratio test (LRT) may provide well-behaved hypothesis tests in situations when the t-statistic does not because the to test statistics are closely related (see,
Phillips and Chen, 2014
).

In this section, we consider the estimation of the predictive regression model from the statistical point of view, motivating the use of the restricted likelihood, which is free of the nuisance intercept parameter and hence able to imitate the likelihood of the no-intercept univariate model with its attendant small curvature. A curvature-related approach to tackle the predictive regression problem for the model was also taken by
Jansson and Moreira, 2006
. Due to the presence of model intercepts
(\mu,\eta)
, we are motivated to seek a likelihood that does not involve the location parameters and yet possesses small curvature properties similar to those of the model with known location parameters. Thus, the restricted likelihood has exactly these properties. In practise, the idea of restricted likelihood was originally proposed by KS (1970) precisely as a means of eliminating the effect of nuisance location parameters when estimating the parameters of the model covariance structure in a linear model.

We focus our attention on the use of the restricted likelihood for carrying inference on
\beta
in the bivariate predictive regression model. Notice that in the particular framework, the restricted likelihood is the exact likelihood of the vector
\big\{(Y_{t}-Y_{t-1})_{t=2}^{n},(X_{t}-X_{t-1})_{t=2}^{n}\big\}
. In practise, the restricted maximum likelihood (REML) estimates are found to be asymptotically efficient. A standard method for testing the two-sided hypothesis,
H_{0}:\beta=0
versus
H_{A}:\beta\neq 0
is the LRT, which compares the log-likelihood evaluated at the unrestricted estimates of the parameters to the log-likelihood evaluated at the parameter estimates obtained under the restriction that the null hypothesis
H_{0}:\beta=0
is true. Then, it is easy to generalize the REML likelihood in two directions that are both of practical interest. One generalization is to the case where the predictor series is a multivariate AR(1) process. The other generalization is to the case where the univariate predictor follows a higher order AR process.

In other words, the inclusion of an intercept in the model causes the likelihood ratio to lose this property, thus pointing to the intercept as the source of the problem. Therefore, this motivates the use of the restricted likelihood, which is free of the nuisance intercept parameter and hence able to imitate the likelihood of the no-intercept univariate model with its attendant small curvature. Thus, we are indeed able to obtain theoretical results that demonstrate the the LRT based on the restricted likelihood (RLRT) has good finite-sample performance for both estimation and inference in this context.

Multivariate Regression

Assume that the data
\big(Y_{1},...,Y_{n},\boldsymbol{X}_{0},...,\boldsymbol{X}_{n}^{\prime}\big)
follow

\displaystyle Y_{t}

\displaystyle=\mu+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+u_{t},

(4.302)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\eta}+\boldsymbol{R}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{t},

(4.303)

where
u_{t}=\boldsymbol{\phi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{v}_{t}+e_{t}
, such that
\big(e_{t},\boldsymbol{v}_{t}^{\prime}\big)^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\mathsf{diag}\left(\sigma_{e}^{2},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}\right)\big)
is an
i.i.d
series and
\boldsymbol{R}
is a
(k\times k)
matrix with all eigenvalues less than unity in absolute value. Define with
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}\equiv\mathsf{Var}(\boldsymbol{v}_{t})
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\equiv\mathsf{Var}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\right)
as

\displaystyle\mathsf{vec}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{X}}\right)=\big(I_{K^{2}}-\boldsymbol{R}\otimes\boldsymbol{R}\big)^{-1}\mathsf{vec}\left(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}\right)

(4.304)

and define with

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}=\left[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{-1}+n(\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{R})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{R})\right]^{-1}\left[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{-1}\boldsymbol{X}_{0}+(\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{R})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{R})\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\right]

(4.305)

Then, the REML log-likelihood up to an additive constant for the model is given by

\displaystyle L_{M}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{n-1}{2}\right)\mathsf{log}\sigma_{e}^{2}-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{e}^{2}}S\left(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{R}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{log}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{X}}|-\frac{n}{2}\mathsf{log}|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}|

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{log}\left|\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{X}}+n(\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{R})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{I}-\boldsymbol{R})\right|

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\frac{1}{2}\left\{\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{0}-\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}\right)^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\boldsymbol{X}}^{-1}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{0}-\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}\right)+\sum_{t=1}^{n}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{t}-\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}-\boldsymbol{R}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}\right)\right]^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}^{-1}\left[\boldsymbol{X}_{t}-\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}-\boldsymbol{R}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}-\hat{\boldsymbol{K}}\right)\right]\right\}

where

\displaystyle S\left(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{R}\right)=\sum_{t=1}^{n}\bigg[Y_{t}^{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\phi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\mu}-\big(\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\phi}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{R}\big)\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\mu}\bigg]^{2}

(4.306)

and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}_{t}^{\mu}=\boldsymbol{X}_{t}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}\ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\mu}=\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}

(4.307)

Remark 25
.

Notice that in the case where
\boldsymbol{R}
is assumed to be diagonal matrix, the predictive regression model is no longer a seemingly unrelated regression (SUR) system and hence OLS will no longer be efficient. However, REML will clearly retain efficiency, no matter what the form of
\boldsymbol{R}
is, thus giving it an advantage in terms of both asymptotic efficiency and power over any OLS-based procedure.

Furthermore, since the dimension of the parameter space is very large in the vector case, it is not feasible to obtain a result such as Theorem 3 in the most general case. However, in the case where
\boldsymbol{R}
is a diagonal matrix and where
\big(\sigma_{e}^{2},\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}\big)
are assumed known with
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{v}
diagonal, we are able to obtain the following result on the finite-sample behaviour of the RLRT for testing
\mathbb{H}_{0}:\boldsymbol{\beta}=\boldsymbol{0}
.

Higher Order Autoregressive Regressors

Let the observed data
\big(Y_{1},...,Y_{n},X_{-p+2},...,X_{n}\big)
follow

\displaystyle Y_{t}

\displaystyle=\mu+\beta X_{t-1}+u_{t},

(4.308)

\displaystyle X_{t}

\displaystyle=\eta+\alpha_{1}X_{t-1}+....+\alpha_{p}X_{t-p}+v_{t}

(4.309)

where
u_{t}=\phi v_{t}+e_{t}
and
\big(e_{t},v_{t}\big)\sim\mathcal{N}\big(\boldsymbol{0},\mathsf{diag}\left(\sigma_{e}^{2},\sigma_{v}^{2}\right)\big)
are an
i.i.d
series. Moreover, assume that all the roots of the polynomial
z^{p}-\sum_{s=1}^{p}z^{p-s}\alpha_{s}
lie within the unit circle. Define with

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Y}^{\mu}=\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{1}\bar{Y},\ \ \ \ \boldsymbol{X}^{\mu}=\bigg[\boldsymbol{X}_{1}-\boldsymbol{1}\bar{X}_{1},...,\boldsymbol{X}_{-p+1}-\boldsymbol{1}\bar{X}_{-p+1}\bigg],

(4.310)

where
X_{i}=\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{1}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}
.

Exercise 1
.

Consider the predictive regression model formulated as below

\displaystyle y_{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)+\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)+u_{t}

(4.311)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}_{t}

\displaystyle=\left(\boldsymbol{I}_{p}-\frac{\boldsymbol{C}_{p}}{n^{\gamma}}\right)\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{v}_{t}

(4.312)

•

Under the null hypothesis we have a predictive regression model with fixed parameter vector which is expresses as
y_{t}=\alpha+\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\beta}+u_{t}
.

•

Under the alternative hypothesis,
\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
\alpha_{t}=\alpha\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)
and
\beta_{t}=\boldsymbol{\beta}\left(\frac{t}{n}\right)
are changing over time.

Therefore, the OLS estimator might not be suitable since there exists no parameter vector
\boldsymbol{\theta}
such that
\mathbb{E}\left(y_{t}|\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}\right)=\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\theta}
almost surely under the alternative hypothesis, where
\boldsymbol{X}_{t-1}=(1,\boldsymbol{x}_{t-1}^{\prime})^{\prime}
such that
\boldsymbol{\theta}=\left(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}
. What about a corresponding IVX estimator? Can it consistently estimate the time-varying parameter
\theta_{t}
given the assumptions of the predictive regression modelling framework?

Provide a suitable asymptotic theory analysis, clearly indicating assumptions and theorems employed for the literature that justify the use of a parametric or nonparametric estimation approach with desirable statistical properties. You can also provide a small Monte Carlo simulation study.

.

\displaystyle=\beta_{1}x_{1,t-1}+\beta_{2}x_{2,t-1}+\beta_{3}x_{3,t-1}+\epsilon_{t}
